Another common example is pages that redirect target users to the login pages of social or mailing sites on the pretext of authentication and presenting the user with similar-looking pages to capture their login information.
*   **Spear Phishing** is a more specialized and directed phishing attack. The primary difference being that spear phishing is directed towards a specific target rather than trying out some luck on a large group of users. The communications are hence customized as per the target user after thorough research. This makes it a more tedious job but may offer a higher success rate. 
    
    ![](images/Figure-5.4.jpg)
    
    **Figure 5.4:** Fake malware scan
    
*   **Scareware**: This involves bombarding the target with false alarms and warnings and providing software solutions for the same. These software are most of the time malware and do more harm than good. A scareware is mainly encountered as pop-ups on websites and contains a link to either a malware downloads or a related website. 
    
    ![](images/Figure-5.5.jpg)
    
    **Figure 5.5:** Scareware
    
*   **Water Holing**: This is another category of a targeted attack. The attacker studies the browsing habits of the target and devices malware delivery through one of the trusted sources. This technique has a higher success rate as the target lets their guard down on familiar websites and hence, making them click on links or tricking them into downloading malware is relatively easy. 
    
    Water holing involves finding vulnerabilities in the websites being visited by the target. Once the vulnerabilities are found, the attacker injects malicious code into the website ready for delivery to the target. Another variant is where the attacker sets up a fake website and brings in the target to operate on it.
    
    ![](images/Figure-5.6.jpg)
    
    **Figure 5.6:** Water Holing
    
*   **Pretexting**: Pretexting involves the impersonation of either a legitimate authority or some scenario to engage the target and obtain information. The extent of pretexting depends upon the scenario and the information needed. This can again be limited to the online virtual world or an impersonation in the physical world. 
    
    For example, an attacker may create a fake social account to engage with the target online. Or the attacker may pretend to be a candidate for an interview to gain physical access to an organizational facility. The core principle of building and maintaining trust is of utmost importance in pretexting since the target cannot be allowed to get suspicious at any time during the hack phase.
    
    Suppose Bob wants to install a hardware keylogger in Adams’ desktop system at home. Bob knows that Adam uses a broadband internet connection and the wiring runs through the area to the distribution point. One fine day, Bob disconnects the connection to Adams’ house by damaging the line. Assuming that Adam has noticed the problem, Bob impersonates the ISP technician and visits Adam on the pretext of carrying out maintenance work. Upon gaining access, he installs the keylogger into the desktop and rectifies the damage he deliberately caused to restore the internet connectivity. Adam is happy since the connection is working again and is totally oblivious to the fact that Bob has now access to all the keystrokes made on his desktop.
    
*   **Baiting**: Baiting involves employing the greed and curiosity quotient of the target users. Baiting attacks are usually not very targeted because they are more of a chance-based scenario where all the onus is on the target taking the bait. Thus, attackers tend to spread out the bait over a large group or area to get the maximum result. The attack can also be carried out in the virtual or the physical world. 
    
    ![](images/Figure-5.7.jpg)
    
    **Figure 5.7:** Baiting with Scareware
    
    In the physical world, an attacker can dish out DVD ROM or pen drives containing malicious content by leaving them in public places and waiting for curious people to use them. Another way could be hiding malicious content in devices being sold on the black market or free samples outside a supermarket.
    
    On the online web, baiting is usually done with the help of flashy banners giving out unbelievable deals or promising intriguing articles. A totally different approach is to scare the target into browsing or downloading content, much in the same way as scareware is used.
    
*   **Identity Theft**: This is very similar to pretexting. The only difference is that identity theft involves impersonating a real person. This is majorly done via forged identity documents. The attacker usually starts with forging or getting hold of a basic document of the target, such as a utility bill. This is followed by a chain of document creation and update iterations till the desired document like a credit card is created. These forged documents can then be used for financial frauds or to simply discredit a person or an organization. 
    
    ![](images/Figure-5.8.jpg)
    
    **Figure 5.8:** Identity theft
    
    **Insider Attacks** occur when one of the authorized users of the system turns into an attacker. Understandably, this is one of the greatest threats to corporate networks. The attacker, in this case, is fully aware of the system internals and is inside the network, thereby already bypassing most of the security measures.
    
    Insider attacks mostly comprise of data theft and backdoor installation in applications in the form of remote management services or credential placement in services not authorized to the user.
    
    Other scenarios of an insider attack could be a system user simply disclosing the security measures and their bypass mechanisms to third parties for some material gains.
    
*   **Eavesdropping and Shoulder Surfing**: Eavesdropping refers to the act of listening to a conversation one is not part of. This could be achieved by either being physically present in the audible distance of the conversation or intercepting the conversation through means such as recorders, network interceptors, keyloggers, and so on. 
    
    Shoulder surfing refers to physically looking over the inputs a person is making on the system while performing some private or confidential activity, like logging in to a service. This is achieved by the attacker being in the line of sight of the input medium, such as a keyboard. An advanced method for this could be using high-resolution cameras in a cybercafé directed towards keyboards.
    
    **Dumpster Diving** is the process of scraping through discards of other users in hopes of finding valuable information. This includes searching through trash, shredded materials, and discarded digital hardware like mobile phones and hard disks for recoverable information.
    
    Dumpster diving often unearths loads of information if the document or hardware lifecycle policy of an organization does not include proper discard rules. Data recovery software can be used to recover data from discarded storage. Old routers and servers can give an idea of the hardware and network setup. Similarly, discarded copies of documents can be reused, like using discarded copies of identity proofs to carry out identity theft or using credit card bills to understand the spending habits of a target and use it while performing a phishing attack on the user.
    
*   **Application Repackaging**: This refers to the act of modifying the original source code by adding custom malicious code and distributing the repackaged application to unsuspecting end users. These applications are usually redistributed on peer-sharing websites or cracked software versions.

# [Tools of Social Engineering](#toc.xhtml#s50a)

The era of the digital age has brought with it endless possibilities of communication and interaction with people at about any place in the world. Riding on this growth, there are numerous tools being used, ranging from naïve ones like simple messaging services to a full-blown data analyzer. Let us discuss some of these here:

*   **Messaging Applications:** The digital world is flooded with messaging applications wherein you could send text messages to anyone. What started as SMS services have become sophisticated with emails and chatting applications like WhatsApp and Hike that provide loads of features like file transfer, location share, and so on. Reaching out to people via these mediums only require the attacker to get hold of the contact information.
*   **Social Media:** A more personalized account about a target can be found out from their social profiles and activities. A lot of corporations scope out candidates on social media to ensure they are a fit match for their work culture. With people sharing all sorts of information freely and quite openly, this medium provides great opportunities to hackers to understand their target, establish connections, and execute the attack within the bounds of this medium itself in several cases.
*   **Disposable (Burner) Phones:** These are devices you can easily dispose of after use without a trace back to the owner. These are bought with hard cash or gift cards and not used for a long duration.  
    
    **Social media** exploitation in phishing attacks is becoming increasingly popular with the deep penetration of social media applications in the masses.
    
    People tend to give out all sorts of details including their interests and whereabouts, which are easily exploitable in phishing attacks.
    
    Adam is an avid social media user and loves posting all his daily activities on social media websites. He has no reservations about publicly displaying his interests and life events. He had recently posted some pictures to his account of a hotel where he had stayed on his trip to a foreign country.
    
    Bob hatches a plan to use this information to his advantage. Using a burner phone, he calls Adam, pretending to be calling from the hotel. To establish trust, he iterates the stay information which was already made public by Adam on social media. Bob now proceeds to notify of some issues in the payment made by Adam during checkout. Bob assures Adam that it’s nothing and he just needs Adam’s credit card information from which the payment was made so that he can get the situation rectified. Adam has little room to suspect and ends up giving out his information. Bob immediately performs some transaction and asks Bob for the confirmation code which Adam unsuspectingly gives.
    
    Adam has no idea that he has been duped until he gets the credit card bill, long after Bob has already disposed of the burner phone.
    
*   **Disposable Numbers:** Just like disposable devices, there are online services that allow you to create disposable numbers and with a little caution, these can be made without any trace back to the actual user. Then there are services that offer disposable numbers to receive messages like OTP and registration codes. This essentially can be used to bypass number verification on a lot of websites. 
    
    ![](images/Figure-5.9.jpg)
    
    **Figure 5.9:** Burner web application
    
*   **Disposable Mailers:** These are temporary mailboxes similar to disposable numbers. These services in principle do not require user registration to maintain privacy and can be used to send/receive emails for a limited time often within a session.
*   **Caller Id Spoofing:** Another method of hiding one’s identity or impersonation is via caller ID spoofing. There are a number of online services that let the caller change the displayed number on the caller ID to a custom number. With services like Truecaller, attackers tend to get extra creative with saving numbers by names of their choices.
*   **Hardware Devices:** Recording devices, spy cameras, etc. help study the behavior of a target. The information gathered from these could also provide leverage to the attacker.
*   **Software:** The sheer volume of information available on various platforms can be overwhelming and impossible for a human to go through in time while the information is still relevant. This is where technology plays a pivotal role. With analysis and relational software blooming, it is both easy and fast to analyze data, create links, host pages, and select the right target and the right strategy. The software here can hence be divided into two broad categories:  
    1.  **Passive:** These are web scrapper and data analysis tools that can be used to gather and process the information to find patterns, meaningful interactions, and relevant details. A customized web crawler can, for example, be used to surf through the complete social media profiles of a target and recursively of any person the target interacts with. There can be notification scripts for when the target becomes active on a platform or on specific actions performed by the target.
    2.  **Active:** Automated bots that create and operate fake social media profiles, automated website copiers, voice spoofing tools, etc. are involved more directly in the hack. These tools take up actions against the information fed and can be very efficient as compared to their human counterparts.

![](images/Figure-5.10.jpg)

**Figure 5.10:** Social Engineering Toolkit

# [Social Engineering Countermeasures](#toc.xhtml#s51a)

*   **Awareness and Attentiveness:** The most effective countermeasure against any social engineering attack is being aware and attentive. Products like credit cards and mobile banking have security guidelines and user reference documents that provide detailed information on how to use these securely and what information is private and what can be divulged.  
    *   Sharing private or confidential information even amongst a close group of individuals immediately puts the information at risk.
    *   A very general principle to understand is that what seems too good to be true probably is not.
*   **Software Countermeasures:** There are a number of extensions for web browsers that guard against pop-up ads and malware downloads. These software guard against malware in the background by giving visual warnings on an encounter with a malware threat. 
    
    ![](images/Figure-5.11.jpg)
    
    **Figure 5.11:** Cloudphish extension in action
    
    A few organizations have also come up with anti-phishing web extensions. These try to identify the content of the website against the domain and also have a repository of any reported phishing websites to warn the users of the same.
    
    ![](images/Figure-5.12.jpg)
    
    **Figure 5.12:** Netcraft information pop-up
    
*   **End-user Training**: Especially necessary for enterprises, periodic end-user training and awareness sessions help keep the buzz about the security policies and their abidance. A one-time mailer about the security policies of the organization hardly solves any issues. Proper interactive training with real-life scenarios is required to let the end-user understand the tricks of the trade.

# [Conclusion](#toc.xhtml#s52a)

Now that we have been familiarized with social engineering, it should not be difficult in understanding the malware payload delivery techniques in the upcoming chapter. Social engineering will be a part of almost all of the hacking methodologies, be it trying to gain insider information of a network or wanting to steal base user credentials to try a privilege escalation on.

However, before moving on to other methodologies, we will first get familiarized with the most common of the attack vectors, the network, in the next chapter.

# [Test Your Understanding](#toc.xhtml#s53a)

1.  **Social engineering can be used for which of the following?** 
    1.  Gathering system passwords
    2.  Gathering the financial information of a user
    3.  Getting a user to click on malicious links
    4.  All of the above
2.  **Dumpster diving involves:** 
    1.  Surfing through discarded items
    2.  Roaming around in search of open Wi-Fi
    3.  Diving in a pile of garbage
    4.  Gathering information by looking over people’s shoulders
3.  **A social engineering toolkit can help:** 
    1.  Copy passwords from the server to a local machine
    2.  Detect the OTP sent on the target mobile device
    3.  Launch social engineering attacks
    4.  Detect phishing attacks
4.  **Which of the following is a way to avoid getting targeted via social engineering on web browsers?** 
    1.  Installing third-party toolbars
    2.  Installing popup ad blockers
    3.  Adding exceptions to visit pages categorized as harmful by the browser
    4.  Clearing the browser cache and local storage daily
5.  **Burner services are used to:** 
    1.  Provide maintenance for cooking gas burners
    2.  Provide services to dispose of sensitive materials
    3.  Provide tools to service a blowtorch
    4.  Provide disposable calling numbers

# [Answers](#toc.xhtml#s54a)

1.  **d**
2.  **a**
3.  **c**
4.  **b**
5.  **d**

# [CHAPTER 6](#toc.xhtml#c06)

# [Scanning Networks](#toc.xhtml#c06)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml#s55a)

Networks form the core of any IT infrastructure as the medium of all communication. Over time, the medium has evolved from being hard-wired to wireless and from transferring a few bits to terabits per second. Exploitable in multiple ways, networks often provide a plethora of information via the data packets traveling through them.

# [Structure](#toc.xhtml#s56a)

We will cover the following topics in this chapter:

*   Computer network concepts
*   Layers of computer networks
*   TCP and UDP connections
*   Tools for network scanning

# [Objectives](#toc.xhtml#s57a)

This chapter aims to provide knowledge of computer network configurations, layers, and components. It deals with the methodologies of getting to know the network an attacker is planning to intrude. By the end of the chapter, you should be able to distinguish between the different types of computer networks, understand their composition, and be familiar with how the network scanning techniques work.

# [Anatomy of a Computer Network](#toc.xhtml#s58a)

Let us assume that an attacker has successfully established a connection to a network. The next logical step would be to discover all that is connected to the network and how in order to prepare a plan of action. A very basic network would likely contain a connection interface and some systems connected via that interface.

![](images/Figure-6.1.jpg)

**Figure 6.1:** Typical local area network

Network scanning is the act of gathering information about the network configurations and services being catered to on the target system. This process, at a minimum, aims to establish the following:

*   Live hosts
*   IP addresses and open ports on the live hosts
*   Services running on the live ports
*   Vulnerabilities in the services running on the ports

There are a few basic terminologies and concepts that we should brush up on before getting any deeper into network sniffing.

*   **Network Interface Controller (NIC):** This is a hardware component in computers that connects the it to a network. This, in turn, allows a system to interact via standards like Ethernet, Wi-Fi, Bluetooth, and so on over protocols such as the Internet Protocol. 
    
    ![](images/Figure-6.2.jpg)
    
    **Figure 6.2:** OSI model
    
*   **OSI Model:** The Open Systems Interconnection model provides a standard layered architecture for any two systems wanting to communicate with each other over standard protocols. The OSI model provides for abstraction over the actual hardware or configuration while at the same time providing interoperability across diverse channels. The OSI model comprises seven layers, each of which handles a specific task in communication and interacts with the layers just adjacent (above and below) to it. 
    
    ![](images/Figure-6.3.jpg)
    
    **Figure 6.3:** TCP/IP stack
    
*   **TCP/IP Protocol Stack:** The TCP/IP suite is a set of protocols that enables communication over different networks with a variety of hardware and software. The stack seldom draws a comparison with the OSI model and can be visualized with four layers. Each layer of the TCP/IP protocol stack encapsulates the data from the previous layer into its abstraction of the **Protocol Data Unit** (**PDU**) and forwards the packet to the next layer. 
    
    ![](images/Figure-6.4.jpg)
    
    **Figure 6.4:** TCP connection and disconnection
    
    ![](images/logo_1.png) The TCP flags indicate the particular state of connection with each flag corresponding to 1 bit of information. The flags are as follows:
    
    1.  **SYN (Synchronization):** The very first communication between the client and the host, this synchronizes the sequence number between the two.
    2.  **ACK (Acknowledgement):** Used to acknowledge the successful reception of a message.
    3.  **FIN (Finish):** Used to request for a graceful connection termination.
    4.  **RST (Reset):** Used to terminate connection if the packet received is not expected.
    5.  **PSH (Push):** Used to communicate to the receiver to process the received packets in buffer without waiting for the buffer to get full.
    6.  **URG (Urgent):** Used to notify the application layer to process the said packet before processing any other packet.
    
*   **User Datagram Protocol:** UDP, or the User Datagram Protocol, is one of the core protocols of the Internet Protocol Suite. It is a connectionless protocol with no requirement of handshaking to establish connections. Rather, there is a simple exchange of a request/response between the client and the server. What this also implies is that there is no service guarantee by the protocol in terms of message delivery, duplication, or order of reception.

![](images/Figure-6.5.jpg)

**Figure 6.5:** UDP connection

Let us now dig a little deeper into connecting all the pieces of information and way of obtaining them for network scanning.

# [Live Host Discovery](#toc.xhtml#s59a)

The very first step for an attacker to perform upon entering the target network is to discover live hosts on the network. These are the systems that an attacker can target once the vulnerabilities are established. The live host discovery is done by querying the network with data packets and analyzing the responses.

**Ping Utility:** Ping is a software utility used to detect the reachability of a system on an IP network. The ping utility employs sending **Internet Control Monitoring Protocol** (**ICMP**) echo requests to a host and receiving the echo reply. In the process, the utility can gauge the availability of a system, the roundtrip time for the packet, and so on.

![](images/Figure-6.6.jpg)

**Figure 6.6:** ICMP Request

Ping Sweep: Executing ping commands for each probable host in a network can be a tireless job and hence, the ping sweep comes into play. Ping sweep is an act of probing multiple systems at once for ping responses. It generally involves passing the IP addresses in incremental loops once you have determined the subnet. For example, a Windows for loop command on incrementing numbers looks like this:

`for %%parameter in (start,step,end) do command`

This can be used to iterate over a network by putting in the start and end of the subnet we wish to Ping. For example, you want to sweep over IP ranges from 10.5.252.1 to 10.5.252.255; the command would look like:

`for %i in (1,1,255) do @ping -n 1 -w 10.5.252.1.%i.`

Similar commands can be constructed for other operating system shells as well.

![](images/logo_1.png) Most modern firewalls have provisions of blocking the ping sweep. There are however ways of extracting information around these as well. Try making use of ARP cache in address resolution.

In addition to the command line, there are several other tools available to execute ping sweeps.

Nmap is a command-line tool that aids in network scanning and provides multiple options for scanning, like IP range, subnet masks, and so on. It can be used to discover hosts and services running on a system in a network by querying different sets of commands to the target system or a set of systems on a network.

![](images/Figure-6.7.jpg)

**Figure 6.7:** Nmap interface

Zenmap is a GUI version of the Nmap tool. It provides an interactive user interface to send packets and analyze the response from the target system or a group of systems on a network.

![](images/Figure-6.8.jpg)

**Figure 6.8:** Zenmap interface

Hping3 is also an open-source network packet generator tool that can be used to query and analyze network traffic.

![](images/Figure-6.9.jpg)

**Figure 6.9:** HPing interface

**Angry IP Scanner** is a cross-platform, GUI-based tool to scan networks. It can scan local networks as well as network ranges over the internet. Additionally, it allows users to export results in multiple formats and also has a command-line interface available.

![](images/Figure-6.10.jpg)

**Figure 6.10:** Angry IP Scanner interface

# [Port Scan and Service Discovery](#toc.xhtml#s60a)

Once the attacker has established the IP addresses, the next step is to establish the open ports and the services running on them. Probing ports and the services on them is made possible because of networking protocols like SSDP which exist to allow service discovery and information on network services. There are various mechanisms and tools that provide varying levels of information. Some of them are as follows:

# [TCP Scan](#toc.xhtml#s61a)

Being a protocol, if a port is running TCP, it is bound to have a certain behavior (true for other protocols as well). TCP scanning uses this behavior to detect those ports that are open and operating on TCP by sending different combinations of protocol stack packets and listening to their responses.

![](images/Figure-6.11.jpg)

**Figure 6.11:** TCP scans

# [UDP Scan](#toc.xhtml#s62a)

UDP being a stateless protocol, there is no three-way handshake. The server responds with an ICMP Port unreachable message in case a UDP port is closed.

![](images/Figure-6.12.jpg)

**Figure 6.12:** UDP scans

Telnet is an application protocol that provides a command-line interface for communication between devices connectable via a network usually over port 23.

![](images/Figure-6.13.jpg)

**Figure 6.13:** Telnet interface for port scan

The Nmap/Zenmap tools discussed in the previous section can also be used to perform port scans.

![](images/Figure-6.14.jpg)

**Figure 6.14:** Zenmap interface for port scan

Hping3 utility is also equipped with commands to perform effective port scans over machines on a network.

![](images/Figure-6.15.jpg)

**Figure 6.15:** Hping3 interface for port scan

# [Banner Grabbing](#toc.xhtml#s63a)

Banner grabbing is used to determine the service or application running on a port by analyzing the responses generated from it. Typical information gathered in banner grabbing includes OS, OS version, application name, version, patch number, and so on.

The process starts with the attacker sending out messages and requests to the target system. Upon receiving the requests, the target system processes them as per their logic and generates responses. The responses expected in these cases are generally error messages or specific file types. Following are the examples of tools used for banner grabbing:

Telnet protocol is one of the most common tools used in banner grabbing. A port 80 query would return details of any web server running on the port. Specific ports can be identified via network scanning methods and then queried by Telnet for banner grabbing.

![](images/Figure-6.16.jpg)

**Figure 6.16:** Telnet interface for banner grabbing

Netcat is another command-line utility that can be used for effective banner grabbing. The query is similar to a Telnet program.

`nc -v <target IP address> <target port>`

![](images/Figure-6.17.jpg)

**Figure 6.17:** Netcat interface for banner grabbing

**ID Serve** is a GUI alternative for server identification over the network. A simple, free, and lightweight utility, the ID Server can perform HTTP and non-HTTP server identification as well as reverse DNS lookup.

![](images/Figure-6.18.jpg)

**Figure 6.18:** ID Serve interface for banner grabbing

# [Network Mapping](#toc.xhtml#s64a)

Once you have gathered a fair knowledge of the target network, it is always a good idea to visualize the network physically. This draws up routes through the network to hop from one system to another and helps prepare the line of attack. The depth of the network diagram depends upon the quantity and quality of scanning and the reconnaissance data gathered.

**Netdiscover** is a simple command-line, ARP-based utility that can be used to query and detect live hosts in a network.

![](images/Figure-6.19.jpg)

**Figure 6.19:** Netdiscover interface for network mapping

**Zenmap** is a GUI wrapper over the popular Nmap utility, which makes it a simple yet effective tool for network mapping in case you are looking for a more interactive mapping solution. The interface allows multiple scanning options like subnet scanning, comparisons between two scans, and profile scans to execute the same scan more than once.

![](images/Figure-6.20.jpg)

**Figure 6.20:** Zenmap interface for network mapping

# [Conclusion](#toc.xhtml#s65a)

Network scanning is often the path-defining activity in a typical infrastructure hack cycle. It yields valuable information on the target network configurations, services, and activities to enable the attacker to perform a well-educated and prepared attempt on intrusion.

Once the information is established, the next logical step is to try and enumerate the services and figure out any weak links in the configuration of the system or the specific services. The next chapter on enumeration deals with the methodologies for efficient enumeration and what can be expected out of a successful enumeration process on a compromised system.

# [Test Your Understanding](#toc.xhtml#s66a)

1.  **Banner grabbing is useful in:** 
    1.  determining the application user groups
    2.  determining the application server environment
    3.  determining the application memory footprint
    4.  determining the application uptime
2.  **UDP can be used to establish reliable communication between two systems.** 
    1.  True
    2.  False
3.  **Telnet can be used for:** 
    1.  banner grabbing
    2.  port scanning
    3.  live host discovery
    4.  all of the above
4.  **An RST flag is sent by the server on receiving an expected request.** 
    1.  True
    2.  False
5.  **Network mapping helps in:** 
    1.  the identification of a line of attack
    2.  the visualization of a target network
    3.  both a & b
    4.  none of the above

# [Answers](#toc.xhtml#s67a)

1.  **b**
2.  **b**
3.  **d**
4.  **b**
5.  **c**

# [CHAPTER 7](#toc.xhtml#c07)

# [Enumeration](#toc.xhtml#c07)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml#s68a)

Simply speaking, enumeration in penetration testing refers to listing down and trying a predefined set of standard actions to gain insight into the system, which may or may not lead to a successful intrusion. A part of the reconnaissance phase, the attacker tries to gather as much information as possible by establishing active connection with the target and probing the services being executed on it.

# [Structure](#toc.xhtml#s69a)

We will cover the following topics in this chapter:

*   Enumeration concepts
*   NetBIOS enumeration
*   SNMP enumeration
*   LDAP enumeration
*   NTP enumeration
*   SMTP enumeration
*   DNS enumeration

# [Objectives](#toc.xhtml#s70a)

This chapter enlists the enumeration phase in a hack cycle. You will be familiarized with the importance of enumeration in the hack cycle. We will also cover the basics of different types of enumeration techniques and their end results. The chapter will also touch base on the common tools used for enumeration.

By the end of the chapter, you should be able to use the information gathered in reconnaissance and network scanning to plan for enumeration phase, have a fair understanding of the different forms of enumeration, and enumerate basic services over a network.

# [Enumeration as a successor to network scan](#toc.xhtml#s71a)

Once the attacker gains access into the target network and establishes an active connection, the next step is to look for standard system services that cater to basic operations like network, sharing, and user access management. Enumeration tries to exploit these services by gathering the configuration information of the system. System defaults and generic dictionaries are often tried as inputs to gather further access into the services like LDAP and WordPress.

To get a sense of what we will be trying to accomplish via enumeration, let us first understand the various forms of enumerations we perform in daily life.

Think of it as shopping in a supermarket. To be effective and quick, it is always preferable to have a shopping list ready. Enhancing its effectiveness is by categorizing the shopping items into groups. Another aspect one might consider is scheduling the trip to the supermarket to make most of some discount schemes or to simply beat the rush. Having a structured approach towards shopping will:

*   Save time while navigating through the different sections of the supermarket since you already know your target sections.
*   Save multiple trips to the same section because of the categorization of items in the list.
*   Save money due to discounts and item rationing.
*   Save you from taking multiple trips to the shopping center because of not remembering some items since you already have a well thought of list.

Enumeration in penetration testing follows the same concept. You have a doorway (connection) opened into the network and have to make the most of it in as little interactions as possible. To do this effectively, you have to be methodical, precise, and thorough.

![](images/Figure-7.1.jpg)

**Figure 7.1:** Enumeration flow

In the subsequent sections, we will cover the various types of enumerations that are possible on a target network or system and the information that can be obtained via them.

# [NetBIOS enumeration](#toc.xhtml#s72a)

**Network Basic Input Output System** or **NetBIOS** is a service (not a protocol) over TCP via NBT protocol, allowing computer systems to communicate over a network via name resolution and registration over the network. It provides the following three services:

*   **Naming Service**: Provides for a name registration and the resolution of constituent systems.
*   **Datagram Service**: Provides for fast, connectionless transmission where reliability is not of utmost importance. Active on UDP port 138.
*   **Session Service**: Used for reliable connection between two systems. Active on TCP port 139.

NetBIOS enumeration can provide the following information:

*   List of systems in a domain
*   File sharing
*   Printer sharing
*   Username
*   Policies
*   Passwords

A NetBIOS name is a combination of 16 ASCII characters. Microsoft has limited the host name to the first 15 characters with the last character being reserved for the NetBIOS Suffix or the NetBIOS End Character. The suffix describes the service or name record type. Some of the common suffixes include:

 
| **NetBIOS Suffix** || **Type** || **Description** |
| <00> || Unique Names || Workstation Service Name |
| <03> || Messenger Service Name |
| <1B> || Domain Master Browser Name |
| <06> || Remote Access Service |
| <20> || File Service |
| <21> || Remote Access Client |
| <1D> || Master Browser |
| <1F> || NetDDE Service |
| <BE> || Network Monitor Agent |
| <BF> || Network Monitor Utility |
| <00> || Group Names || Workstation Service |
| <1C> || Domain Group Name |
| <1D> || Master Browser Name |
| <1E> || Browser Service Elections |

**Table 7.1:** NetBIOS suffixes

**SuperScan:** SuperScan is a free, connect-based port scanning tool to detect TCP and UDP ports on target systems and enumerating them to obtain network information.

![](images/Figure-7.2.jpg)

**Figure 7.2:** SuperScan port scanning report

The SuperScan scanning interface provides a bunch of scanning options based on the IP address/hostname or a range of addresses. The results can also be viewed in the HTML format.

![](images/Figure-7.3.jpg)

**Figure 7.3:** SuperScan host scanning interface

The host and service discovery tab can be used to perform a scan of all UDP and TCP ports in the range of 0 to 65535.

![](images/Figure-7.4.jpg)

**Figure 7.4:** SuperScan host and service discovery interface

Superscan also provides the ability to enumerate a Windows system via the Windows enumeration tab. This interface has a rich set of options ranging from NetBIOS to user session enumerations on a Windows machine.

![](images/Figure-7.5.jpg)

**Figure 7.5:** SuperScan Windows enumeration interface

The tools tab of SuperScan provides features like hotname/IP lookup, PING, ICMP traceroute, zone transferm HTTP HEAD and GET requests, HTTPS GET request, Whois lookup, and so on.

![](images/Figure-7.6.jpg)

**Figure 7.6:** SuperScan tools tab interface

`Nbtstat`: This is a Windows command line utility for querying the NetBIOS information over TCP/IP protocol statistics, NetBIOS name tables, and NetBIOS name cache.

![](images/Figure-7.7.jpg)

**Figure 7.7:** Nbtstat interface

`Nbtstat -c:` It is the most commonly used variant of the utility which lists out the content NetBIOS name cache.

![](images/Figure-7.8.jpg)

**Figure 7.8:** Nbtstat -c interface

**Nbtstat -a**: It is used to query the NetBIOS name table of the remote system specified in the IP address appended to the command. It effectively lists our NetBIOS names that correspond to the NetBIOS applications running on the target system.

![](images/Figure-7.9.jpg)

**Figure 7.9:** Nbtstat -a interface

**Hyena:** This is a Windows Explorer styled GUI interface to perform various types of enumerations on the Windows machines and networks.

![](images/Figure-7.10.jpg)

**Figure 7.10:** Hyena interface

**Winfingerprint**: This is a Windows-based host/network enumeration tool capable of performing SMB, TCP, UDP, RPC, ICMP, and SNMP scans.

![](images/Figure-7.11.jpg)

**Figure 7.11:** Winfingerprint interface

# [SNMP Enumeration](#toc.xhtml#s73a)

**Simple Network Management Protocol** or **SNMP** is an application layer protocol providing a common language for network devices to exchange network management information within a local or a wide area network. The SNMP environment consists of the following four components:

*   **SNMP Agent:** The piece of software monitoring a hardware or service.
*   **Network Nodes:** The network devices or services being monitored.
*   **SNMP Manager:** This is the central command center for the SNMP agents. The agents report back to this system at periodic intervals with the monitoring information. The manager can then take actions based on this information, depending on its capabilities.
*   **Management Information Base:** The MIB is a database text file (`.mib`) that contains a list and description of all objects that can be managed by SNMP.

![](images/Figure-7.12.jpg)

**Figure 7.12:** SNMP Stack

SNMP works via commands and messages transported over UDP or TCP/IP. The basic commands used are as follows:

*   `GET`: The SNMP manager requests for information from the SNMP agent.
*   `GETNEXT`: An SNMP manager requests data continuously until all data with the SNMP agent is discovered.
*   `GETBULK`: Introduced in SNMPv2c, this message is used to retrieve a large data set at one go.
*   `SET`: Used by SNMP manager to set the value of an object on the SNMP agent.
*   `RESPONSE`: Message sent as a reply by the SNMP agent to the message received from the SNMP manager.
*   `TRAP`: These are messages sent from the SNMP agent to the SNMP manager, indicating a fault.
*   `INFORM`: Introduced in SNMPv2c, this is an acknowledgement message from the SNMP manager to the SNMP client informing the receipt of a TRAP message.

**OpUtils:** It is an IP address and switch port management tool that helps in network monitoring, management, and debugging. The easy-to-use interface and analytics provide deep insights into the target network for a hacker. It is available in free and paid versions, with the free version providing most of the enumeration tools.

![](images/Figure-7.13.jpg)

**Figure 7.13:** OpUtils dashboard

**MIB Explorer:** The MIB explorer is a management tool for the SNMP network and SNMP enabled devices. The tool enables sending and receiving SNMP request packets and processes them according to its rule engine. It can additionally support IPv6, traceroutes, and device snapshot creation.

![](images/Figure-7.14.jpg)

**Figure 7.14:** MIB Explorer interface

# [LDAP enumeration](#toc.xhtml#s74a)

**Lightweight Directory Access Protocol** or **LDAP** works over TCP/IP to access information from the distributed directory information services. An LDAP directory is a set of objects containing the information hierarchy. This can contain information ranging from usernames, roles, organizational hierarchy, passwords, and so on.

LDAP services can sometimes be queried anonymously by an attacker to determine the valid users, addresses, departmental details, and so on.

**JXplorer:** An open-source LDAP browser, JXplorer can be used to perform all basic LDAP operations. It provides a GUI-based configurable view to easily view and perform updates on the LDAP directory along with full support for SSL and SASL authentication via a custom java SSL socket factory.

![](images/Figure-7.15.jpg)

**Figure 7.15:** JXplorer interface

Newer versions of the tool use the SUN DSML/JNDI provider to allow a connection to DSML-enabled directories via anonymous binds.

![](images/Figure-7.16.jpg)

**Figure 7.16:** JXplorer LDAP/DSML connection interface

**LDAP Admin:** LDAP Admin is a free client to access, search, create, modify, and delete LDAP databases and their objects via Windows machines. It is also capable of performing recursive operations on directory trees, schema browsing, password management, and a variety of other features. Newer versions also include support for Samba domains and SSL support.

![](images/Figure-7.17.jpg)

**Figure 7.17:** LDAP Admin interface

# [NTP enumeration](#toc.xhtml#s75a)

**Network Time Protocol** or **NTP** is a clock synchronization network protocol over UDP. The NTP synchronizes time between two systems, an NTP server and an NTP client, by the clock synchronization algorithm which is a combination of intersect algorithm, a modification of the Marzullo’s algorithm. NTP uses a layered hierarchy of systems from Stratum (level) 0 to Stratum 15. A Stratus 16 system is said to be unsynchronized.

The NTP can be used to gather the following information about a network:

*   List of hosts connected to an NTP server
*   Client information like IP addresses, hostnames, and OS
*   Internal IP addresses of the NTP server in a demilitarized zone

![](images/Figure-7.18.jpg)

**Figure 7.18:** NTP architecture

# [NTP enumeration commands](#toc.xhtml#s76a)

`ntptrace`: Traces a chain of NTP hosts back to the master time source.

`ntpdc`: Used to query the NTP daemon (`ntpd`) about its current state and to request changes to the state.

`ntpq`: Monitors the NTP daemon (`ntpd`) and determines its performance.

**Wireshark**: Wireshark is a free and open-source packet analyzer. It is one of the most popular packet analysis, troubleshooting, and filtering tool out in the market. Some of the features include live packet capture and offline analysis, wide coverage of network protocols, multi-platform support, VoIP analysis, compression operations on the fly, report exports, and so on.

Wireshark works by activating the promiscuous mode of the supporting NIC, thereby enabling interception of all network traffic.

![](images/Figure-7.19.jpg)

**Figure 7.19:** Wireshark Interface

# [SMTP enumeration](#toc.xhtml#s77a)

**Simple Mail Transfer Protocol** or **SMTP** is an application layer protocol used widely to send email communication over the internet. It relies on a mail exchange server directing the mails via Domain Name Servers. Unlike POP3 and IMAP protocols, SMTP cannot receive mail communication. SMTP has the following commands available:

*   **HELO**: First SMTP command that starts the conversation.
*   **EHLO**: First SMTP command that starts the conversation when the underlying server is using the Extended SMTP protocol.
*   **MAIL** **FROM**: Identifies the sender of the mail.
*   **RCPT TO**: Identifies the receiver/s of the mail.
*   **SIZE**: Used to indicate the estimated size of the incoming mail.
*   **DATA**: Begins the transfer of the mail content.
*   **VRFY**: Used to verify a mail address on a server.
*   **TURN**: Invert client and server roles without reconnecting.
*   **AUTH**: Used to perform client authentication based on the username and password.
*   **RSET**: Used to terminate an ongoing transmission without terminating the SMTP connection.
*   **EXPN**: Used to expand the mailing list to obtain the actual recipient addresses.
*   **QUIT**: Called on to terminate the SMTP conversation.

The `VRFY`, `EXPN,` and `RCPT TO` commands can be used to enumerate valid users on the SMTP server.

**Telnet**: A user can query the SMTP server which usually runs on port 25 via Telnet to open a communication channel. Once the communication is established, a simple `VRFY` command can be used to check whether the user is a valid user or not. For a valid user, the `VRFY` command returns the message codes 250, 251, or 252. A response code of 550 means it is an invalid user.

![](images/Figure-7.20.jpg)

**Figure 7.20:** Telnet Interface for SMTP Enumeration

NetScan Pro: The SMTP Email Generator tool in *NetScanTools Pro* simulates the process of sending an email message through an SMTP server. All popular email header parameters, including confirm/urgent flags, can be extracted along with support for simple SMTP authentication as well as `STARTTLS` authentication with a username and password. It is also possible to submit email attachments and log the email session. The Email Relay Testing Tool in *NetScanTools Pro* conducts relay testing by interacting with an SMTP server.

![](images/Figure-7.21.jpg)

**Figure 7.21:** NetScan Pro interface for an SMTP enumeration

# [DNS enumeration](#toc.xhtml#s78a)

**Domain Name System** or DNS is a hierarchical naming system for computer systems, services, and other resources connected to the internet. The DNS records contain a host of information ranging from IP addresses to mail server records, TXT records, and so on. These records divulge information like DNS server names, machine names, usernames, hosts, IP addresses, physical information, and so on.

Dig: Dig is a command line tool for querying the Domain Name System. It queries the “A” records in DNS by default. The following parameters can be used to simplify the output:

*   `nocmd` – Removes the +cmd options output.
*   `noall` – Removes extra headers, flags, time information, message size, and so on.
*   `answer` – Tells Dig to return the answer section (the “juicy” part of the output).

![](images/Figure-7.22.jpg)

**Figure 7.22:** Dig enumeration interface

Further options can be configured to obtain other DNS records as well. These are generally the name of the record appended at the end of the Dig command. For example, for a nameserver record, the command would be as follows:

`dig <domain name> ns`

![](images/Figure-7.23.jpg)

**Figure 7.23:** Dig DNS enumeration interface

**NSLookup:** One of the most common DNS enumeration tools, `nslookup` is actually a command line administration tool for DNS. It provides a host of useful options for DNS lookups and works with two modes, interactive and non-interactive modes. The interactive mode is invoked by simply querying the `nslookup` command without any parameters or by invoking it with the first argument as minus sign (-) and the hostname of the IP address as the second argument. It opens up an `nslookup` prompt which can be used to query the DNS records for the host.

![](images/Figure-7.24.jpg)

**Figure 7.24:** NSLookup options

The non-interactive mode is invoked by giving the hostname or IP address as the first parameter and the query given as command line arguments.

![](images/Figure-7.25.jpg)

**Figure 7.25:** NSLookup DNS enumeration interface

**Whois:** The command line utility to communicate with the WHOIS server, `whois` command can also be used to enumerate DNS records of a hostname or IP address from the central whois server.

![](images/Figure-7.26.jpg)

**Figure 7.26:** Whois DNS enumeration interface

# [Conclusion](#toc.xhtml#s79a)

Enumeration tends to give out a fair idea of the system composition and configuration. It can also lead to a lot of vulnerabilities getting discovered in the system of its own accord or inherited from the individual components.

This chapter effectively completes the information gathering phase of the hack cycle. In the next few chapters, we will deep dive into the tools and techniques used to pivot the information we have gathered in the reconnaissance phase and break into information systems at various levels, starting with an analysis of the vulnerabilities discovered in the course of the hack cycle till now.
