    3.  Collecting the user activity information via enterprise policies to govern mobile devices
    4.  Geofencing application to monitor device presence in a network

# [Answers](#toc.xhtml#s181a)

1.  **c**
2.  **d**
3.  **b**
4.  **c**
5.  **a**

# [CHAPTER 15](#toc.xhtml#c15)

# [Hacking Cloud, IoT, and OT Platforms](#toc.xhtml#c15)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml#s182a)

Cloud and **Internet of Things** (**IoT**) can be counted in a few of the advancements in computing that have had a mass impact on both technological advancement and consumer behavior. Not only have these revolutionized communication and IT infrastructure management, but helped bring technology to the masses at a rapid pace. **Operation Technology** (**OT**) forms an important part of this ecosystem, allowing organizations to monitor and control these large and distributed systems centrally with minimal effort.

# [Structure](#toc.xhtml#s183a)

We will cover the following topics in this chapter:

*   Cloud computing concepts
*   Cloud service and deployment models and popular vendors
*   Virtualization and containers
*   Threats and attacks
*   IoT concepts
*   IoT threats and attack vectors
*   OT and IIoT
*   Tools to aid in cloud and IoT hacking

# [Objectives](#toc.xhtml#s184a)

In this chapter, we will be analyzing the cloud and IoT platforms with respect to the challenges they face in terms of security from the most basic level to the most robust enterprise architectures. To do this, we will start with the basic concepts and architecture of cloud and IoT systems, their adaption into existing infrastructures, weak points, and exploitation. We will additionally touchpoint on virtualization and container concepts that form the backbone of these structures at a scale.

# [Cloud Computing](#toc.xhtml#s185a)

Perhaps one of the biggest buzzwords of the software industry, cloud computing refers to a network providing resources over the internet. Speaking in a crude sense, the cloud is a cluster of servers sitting remotely, managed by a cloud service provider for a fee. The applications act as tenants on the cloud and the application owners no longer need to set up or maintain the infrastructure for the applications.

![](images/Figure-15.1.jpg)

**Figure 15.1:** Cloud computing

Thus, for enterprises, cloud computing provides the following major benefits over traditional practices:

*   Infrastructure setup and maintenance operations are managed by the cloud provider rather than the organization. This enables the enterprises to be free of the hassles of maintaining complex hardware for operations.
*   Availability is guaranteed by the cloud service providers with robust mechanisms for low latency experience. The service is independent of the single point of failure and usually distributed across multiple locations.
*   Scaling up and down is generally not an issue with cloud services with most cloud providers giving options of scaling on demand. This reduces cost as well as downtime due to resource choking.

The majority of cloud service providers offer many more services as add-ons like reporting, analytics, messaging platforms, and so on. which can be readily integrated with applications on the platform to reduce the go-to-market time of the application as well as the uniformity of the application stack.

# [Cloud Infrastructure](#toc.xhtml#s186a)

![](images/Figure-15.2.jpg)

**Figure 15.2:** Cloud infrastructure hierarchy

The cloud, in the simplest form, is a layer of hardware and its controlling software and applications exposed at various levels meeting a wide variety of needs. At its core, the cloud infrastructure has its physical components in the forms of servers, data centers, and network connections. The data centers are usually distributed across multiple geographical locations for distributed redundancy and low latency across different locations.

On top of the hardware lies the computing infrastructure. These contain virtualization solutions that help isolate and manage resources for different requirements. This forms the **Infrastructure as a Service** (**IaaS**) part of the cloud and the containers can be directly leased out to the clients to be utilized as their own infrastructure to build upon.

The platform and storage tier on top of IaaS forms the **Platform as a Service** (**PaaS**) layer, which provides application servers and storage platforms for the clients.

The top layer within the cloud consists of the applications and services provided by the cloud providers to be readily integrated or used standalone. This tier forms the **Software as a Service** (**SaaS**) part of the cloud. A cloud provider generally uses this layer to provide value-added services to its customers like notification services, readily available functions, web services, and so on.

The ability of the cloud to provide all three service models over the same infrastructure seamlessly is what makes it a unique setup and distinguishes it from the traditional distributed computing solutions.

# [Cloud Deployment Models](#toc.xhtml#s187a)

The cloud deployment model decides how and to whom the cloud services are available. There are quite a few cloud deployment models:

1.  Public **c**loud is where cloud services are available for public use over a network with the latter having no control over the infrastructure location.
2.  Private **c**loud is deployed where an enterprise needs a more controlled environment in terms of usage and security. A private cloud is usually commissioned by an individual enterprise for internal purposes. 
    
    ![](images/Figure-15.3.jpg)
    
    **Figure 15.3:** Cloud deployment models
    
3.  Hybrid **c**loud comes into the picture where an organization plans to keep its confidential data within a private cloud and still allow interaction with the public cloud for some of its services.
4.  Community **c**loud is configured to serve a specific set of customers working in collaboration. This deployment model is especially useful for organizations working under the same set of regulations or governance as in the case of compliance requirements for a specific country or region. The deployment for this can be managed internally by the participating tenants or by a third-party provider.

# [Virtualization](#toc.xhtml#s188a)

A major problem with cloud providers and the tenants lies in the underutilization of resources. As a tenant, an enterprise would want to utilize all resources it is paying for. A cloud provider would also on the other hand want all its hardware to be used optimally.

![](images/Figure-15.4.jpg)

**Figure 15.4:** Physical vs Virtual machine

The solution to this problem is offered via virtualization practices that work to abstract the physical resources of a computing environment to work as logical resources. A single physical machine would hence be able to host multiple logical machines, each running within its own logical boundaries or containers. Each of these would have the capacity to work independently, having its own operating system and software.

Such an environment enables a cloud provider to dedicate virtual machines to a tenant rather than a physical machine. This results in a higher redundancy at a lower cost. The physical machines too end up being used to their maximum capacity as they are offered in smaller blocks rather than one huge machine.

For a cloud tenant, there is no need to hire complete machines, but virtual machines as per their requirement. The machines could be spun up on-demand, as per the requirement with the necessary computing power. This saves an organization a lot of cost in terms of computing spends.

An important point to make here is that virtualization is not necessarily limited to complete virtual machines but can be on different levels.

**Hardware Virtualization** is done at the machine hardware level to enable division and monitoring of hardware resources at the most basic level.

**Operating System Virtualization** is implemented by installing the virtualization platform on a host operating system rather than on the hardware.

**Server Virtualization** is done to divide a server machine into multiple smaller server instances by installing the virtualization solution directly on the server machine.

**Storage Virtualization** aims to combine multiple storage devices over a network to provide a virtually large, single storage.

**Network Virtualization** is done with the aim of providing a singly administered virtual network over multiple networks or to provide multiple virtual networks over a single physical network.

*VMWare*, *Oracle VM*, *ESXi*, *Hyper-V,* and *vSphere* are some of the popular virtualization providers in the market.

# [Containers](#toc.xhtml#s189a)

A container is an important concept in cloud computing when it comes to deploying applications over a system. A container is a form of operating system virtualization that isolates a process hosted within it and its access to the system resources. This enables multiple applications running on a single operating system in complete isolation from each other while having dedicated system resources allocated. This is especially useful in the case of applications that require security or dedicated resources. The isolation also helps keep applications running in different containers free from any ill effects like resource hogging and deadlocks due to resource scarcity.

![](images/Figure-15.5.jpg)

**Figure 15.5:** Virtualization vs Containerization

*Docker*, *AWS Fargate*, *Amazon ECS*, *Portainer* are a few popular container solutions.

A container is usually not deployed alone but in clusters when supporting enterprise applications. To manage deployment and other life cycle events in synchronization with each other, enterprises use solutions like *Kubernetes* and *Portainer*.

# [NIST Cloud Computing Reference Architecture](#toc.xhtml#s190a)

The NIST cloud computing reference architecture defines the basic conceptual model for defining cloud entities and their interactions. It defines five major actors in a cloud transaction:

*   Cloud provider
*   Cloud broker
*   Cloud carrier
*   Cloud consumer
*   Cloud auditor

![](images/Figure-15.6.jpg)

**Figure 15.6:** NIST cloud computing

# [Cloud computing threats](#toc.xhtml#s191a)

An important aspect to understand before we dive into the threats that a cloud environment faces is that at its core, the applications running on the cloud are more or less equivalent to the ones deployed on-premise in terms of technology stack and environment. What changes is the deployment environment, which might include packaging and network interactions. Beneath all this, the threats that are true for an on-premise application will still be relevant for cloud-based applications. The cloud deployment would not solve an XSS attack vulnerability by itself.

What this implies is that the threat vectors of a cloud application would majorly resemble an on-premise application with differentiation in deployment, infrastructure, and network levels. We will hence be covering attack vectors that are able to leverage the cloud and not application-level vulnerabilities like session hijacking.

Let us now first understand what specialized threats are introduced once THE cloud infrastructure comes into play:

1.  **Data Privacy and protection** is the top concern for any application or enterprise. With the data no longer residing at closely guarded, on-premise servers, the risk suddenly shoots up. In public cloud deployments, this can be a major issue since the enterprise or application would have no control over the data location or actual protection policies. What escalates this concern further is that one point of the breach in the cloud storage layer would implicate multiple applications hosted on the cloud that use its storage services.
2.  **Service abuse** occurs when, due to a lack of governance or review, an attacker is able to abuse cloud services to host malicious content like phishing websites, botnets, or libraries. Due to the nature of cloud services and the easily available add-ons, it has become quite easy for hackers to perform these activities while remaining anonymous.
3.  **Inadequate application security** is also a major concern around cloud deployments due to factors like improper or inadequate understanding of cloud infrastructure or the deployment solution. It often leaves the developers and administrators with a false sense of security. A very common example of this is when developers leave databases open to access from the internet assuming that the IP of the server would be hard to brute force or simply due to ignorance, thereby becoming soft targets for ransomware attacks.

**Ransomware attacks** are specially formulated attacks where the attacker takes control of the data on the target system and encrypts the same using a cryptographic function with the encryption keys being known only to the attacker. This effectively locks out the actual system owner from the system. To decrypt the data or in exchange for the encryption key, the hacker demands monetary benefits usually in the forms of cryptocurrencies.

Ransomware attacks are mostly time-bound with the attacker threatening to delete the data post expiry of a fixed time to push a sense of urgency and make sure that there is no brute force attempts to decrypt the data.

*WannaCry, Locky, Jigsaw* are a few of the ransomware to hit systems across the globe.

# [Cloud attack vectors](#toc.xhtml#s192a)

As previously discussed, the cloud environment has its own threats introduced due to the overall architecture and usage. The same is true for attack vectors, with the cloud applications inheriting most of the attack vectors from the web and server application stack and then having their own attack vectors arising due to the unique attack surface that the cloud offers to an attacker. We have classified these vectors into four specialized categories as follows:

1.  **DOS/DDOS attacks** on the cloud can be directed against a single application or the cloud provider as a whole. On a cloud, this can again be directed to a specific tier of the cloud like IaaS, PaaS, or SaaS. Performing denial of service on a cloud platform is usually difficult and involves attackers deploying botnets across multiple systems to participate in the distributed attack. A major area of concern here for the application owners is the auto-scaling feature. A DOS/DDOS attack could trigger the auto-scaling of resources on the cloud and ramp up costs in infrastructure making the attack much more effective.
2.  **Malware injection** is used to deceive a cloud service user into interacting with an impersonating service. This is achieved by the injection of malware at the infrastructure, platform, or instance level and slipping it past the security checks. DNS-level attacks are also used to complement these attacks.
3.  **Cross-cloud attacks** occur when an attacker is able to identify the connection or movement of data from public clouds to private clouds or on-premise infrastructure. This usually occurs when enterprises or application owners use hybrid models for deployment or use solutions to enable communication across different clouds. For example, a developer sitting at an enterprise facility interacts with the cloud server stack for monitoring or file uploads causing a connection to be opened between the enterprise network and the cloud server. An attacker can use this channel to transfer malware from the cloud to the on-premise infrastructure.
4.  **Side-channel attacks** are devised to gain insider knowledge of the deployment architecture of a cloud service to attack an application hosted on its virtual environment. The attacker tries to deploy his own services on an equivalent virtual machine in the cloud service to understand the server environment or cryptographic functions securing the server. This is pretty common for attackers wanting to attack web applications deployed on shared hosting by identifying the hosting providers and spinning up their own applications on the same provider infrastructure to gain crucial server configurations and functions. This information can then be used to devise more effective and targeted attacks on the target applications.

# [Internet of Things (IoT)](#toc.xhtml#s193a)

IoT or Internet of Things is a system of physical non-computing devices connected over a network with the devices transmitting various information and metrics to other devices in the network or a centralized command center with the aim of giving a seamless user experience to the end-user. IoT networks can be as simple as appliances like refrigerators connected to mobile devices via an application. The devices could then be monitored and controlled remotely. Stepping up the complexity are networks like numerous sensors in a connected vehicle. These sensors stream data to the vehicle dashboard and as well as remotely located control centers to provide data for vehicle performance measurement.

An example of even larger configurations includes systems operating on the levels of SCADA systems to monitor and supervise large groups of facilities or even larger clusters of devices and sensors.

![](images/Figure-15.7.jpg)

**Figure 15.7:** IoT architecture

# [IoT Architecture](#toc.xhtml#s194a)

IoT systems have varying configurations based on the use cases. The same is true for the scale at which the system is deployed. However, from very basic to the most complex IoT systems, there are four layers that come together to make the system operational:

1.  **Things** are at the center of the IoT universe. These are the devices that are connected to a network. A “*thing*” in an IoT system can be any non-computing device ranging from a door lock to earthmover machines with **sensors and controllers**. The sensors work to collect event data from the devices. The data is then sent to the control centers where it is used in calculating system metrics to provide actionable intelligence. The controllers can then transform the actions and events generated by the command center into actual physical events on the devices.
2.  **Gateways** reside between the device layer and the rest of the system, transforming device event data into transmissible signals and forwarding the relevant data to the command center and vice versa. Another important aspect of gateways is to maintain information security for all transference of data to and from the devices and the IT infrastructure.
3.  **Edge analytics** forms an optional part of the IoT infrastructure, commissioned when the system needs real-time processing of events and. Edge systems are placed nearer to the devices, often within the same network to reduce network latency. These systems are used to crunch small volumes of data locally and provide quick responses and actions on the basis of these calculations.
4.  **Data center** forms the last layer of the system, collecting all meaningful events and having systems provide storage, processing, and analytics to the system. With the cloud in the picture, the IoT systems do not require physical data center setups and can work on top of the cloud services that optimize costs as well as provide better availability.

# [IoT communication models](#toc.xhtml#s195a)

A very important aspect of IoT systems is the way the devices communicate with each other and their command-and-control centers. A few of these communication models are as follows:

*   **Device-to-Device Model** wherein different devices are connected to each other directly via protocols like Bluetooth. Bluetooth beacons interacting with cell phones to provide building navigation or perimeter services is an example of such a communication model.
*   **Device-to-Cloud Model** in which the devices directly communicate with cloud services, rendering some information in the raw form. A common example of this model of communication is the CCTV cameras providing their feed via cloud services.
*   **Device-to-Gateway Model** where the devices communicate to a gateway that filters or pre-processes data packets and then sends them over to the command or storage centers. Smart highway systems monitoring speeding vehicles is an example of this model.
*   **Third-party Data Sharing Model** is used when the data from IoT devices is shared by the device owner with third-party services that in turn provide services over them. An application of this model is with connected cars where collision detection can directly alert emergency services.

# [Threats to IoT Networks](#toc.xhtml#s196a)

The IoT networks, like other networks, are prone to attacks like the man in the middle attack, denial of service, etc. But perhaps the most critical of the threats to an IoT network are as follows:

*   **Standardization** of connectivity, communication, and interaction in an IoT environment is a big challenge considering the wide variety of devices and networks that come together to form it. Adding to this complexity is the go-to-market race among manufacturers that results in sub-standard security and easily available exploits.
*   **Component takeover** is when an attacker gains access and control to a component or the whole network. With the volume of devices connected to the IoT, a device takeover can spell disaster for the network or client. Imagine a door lock not raising an alarm on being picked or an engine reporting the wrong wear and tear metrics.
*   **Information disclosure** is again a big threat to IoT systems because of the sheer volume of information that flows through these systems. Any data breach or leak in the system can result in large volumes of data being stolen and used for malicious purposes and major embarrassment and possible lawsuits for the service providers.

# [IoT attack vectors](#toc.xhtml#s197a)

IoT inherently works on the ease of connectivity among devices, sensors, gateways, and networks. Information flow forms the core of any such network and maintaining a seamless channel for the same is a challenge. An equally challenging task is to secure such a network that is meant to be open and easily connectible. Attackers take advantage of this very fact and go after one or more of the possible attack vectors.

*   Devices or Things are often the most easily accessible parts of the IoT network. With so many configurations and architectures, securing all devices over an IoT network is a herculean task.
*   Network is another point of concern for system administrators since all the devices need to communicate over the network and each device configuration uses its own flavor of connectivity. While one device might prefer connecting via Wi-Fi, another might connect over Bluetooth and yet another might transfer data via a cellular network.
*   Edge systems have access to most of the data for instantaneous analysis. These are hence popular targets since these usually lie outside the heavily-guarded data centers.
*   Cloud platforms that provide administration portals along with monitoring and viewing portals can be exploited similarly to web and application servers to gain access to the information and the system as a whole.
*   Third-party systems are often integrated with IoT systems for data sharing and analytics purposes. These in turn can be exploited to extract connection and integration information to gain access to the information or the main system itself.

# [Operational Technologies (OT) and Industrial Internet of Things (IIoT)](#toc.xhtml#s198a)

With the amount of automation and scaling, it becomes nearly impossible to monitor large systems manually. With dashboards increasing from a few to thousands and operational metrics becoming more and more granular and critical, it was but an obvious shift from manual operations towards more scalable operational technologies using hardware and software to monitor and control systems, processes, and complete infrastructures.

Majorly found in a resource-intensive environment, the OT networks are not typically exposed to the internet and hence used to go soft on the security implementation. However, with the rapid rise in mobility and demand for greater connectivity, this isolation is not always possible. Even in silo networks, the touchpoints of the systems warrant a robust security implementation, especially since these systems usually control critical functions ranging from assembly-line robots to nuclear reactor cooling systems.

At their core, the network can be visualized as a specialized IoT network relying on a large number of hardware sensors and software interfaces to collect operational information in real-time and performing meaningful operations on the basis of these inputs. The purpose of these systems is always to manage industrial operations and not administrative operations.

The **Industrial Control Systems** (**ICS**) are OT systems categorically designed for mission-critical systems requiring high availability and precision like electricity grids.

**Industrial Automation** (**IA**) is another aspect of OT wherein the operation aspects of handling processes and machinery are taken care of by systems like robots and software solutions. **Process Control Networks** (**PCS**) is the communication layer used to transfer information and signals across the OT networks, between the machines, software components, and the measurement and control systems.

**Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition** (**SCADA**) systems are often used to manage the ICS systems. These provide centralized command and control system with an intuitive graphical representation of the system in real-time. SCADA systems are widely used in the administration of electricity grids, smart city infrastructures, smart highways, and so on.

![](images/Figure-15.8.jpg)

**Figure 15.8:** Components of typical SCADA system

The systems discussed here, working in synchronization with each other, form the core of the Industrial Internet of Things. The sensors of IIoT are placed on or near the OT devices like remote processing units, robots, switches, and so on.

# [OT and IIoT attack vectors](#toc.xhtml#s199a)

The OT attack vectors are quite similar to the attack vectors in IoT. These include:

1.  Poor access control on devices
2.  Network interfacing without security parameters
3.  Missing network segmentation
4.  Patch ignorance
5.  Insecure gateways
6.  Lack of system hardening

# [Cloud, IoT, and OT hacking tools](#toc.xhtml#s200a)

Two very important facts to highlight before we get into some tools used for cloud and IoT hacking are as follows:

1.  The cloud, IoT, and OT systems at their core are made up of information systems comprising of web and application servers just like other computing systems on the internet.
2.  The components interact with other components via networks like other computing devices, machines, etc. These networks can be wired or wireless and follow different communication protocols.

The novelty lies in the scale and processing power and distribution of infrastructure. For an attacker, the difference between hacking a traditional computing system and a cloud or IoT system would hence be in navigating the system and, more importantly, getting inside the system. The tools we will be discussing here hence would revolve around reconnaissance of the cloud, IoT, and OT systems.

1.  **Internet search engines**: Google, to be precise, has a huge indexing of online servers and publicly accessible information that is mainly left open due to misconfigurations or overlooking by the system administrators. In fact, Google Hacking is a whole area of study for researchers to extract information from Google indexes via curated queries available on the advanced search options of Google. 
    
    ![](images/Figure-15.9.jpg)
    
    **Figure 15.9:** Google advanced search portal
    
2.  **Cloud and IoT search engines**: There are specialized search engines dedicated to indexing the cloud and IoT infrastructure. These can at times contain publicly available servers that might or might not be intended for public access. A few of these search engines are *shodan.io*, *thingful.net,* and *iotcrawler.eu*. 
    
    ![](images/Figure-15.10.jpg)
    
    **Figure 15.10:** Shodan querying for public Elasticsearch servers
    
3.  **DNS lookup tools**: The DNS lookup tools can provide a lot of information about a cloud server domain or IoT network. Cloud services often require domain owners to provide proof of ownership via DNS entries, mostly TXT records, and administrators tend to leave these records even after verification. This can enable attackers to identify integrations and plan their exploits accordingly. 
    
    ![](images/Figure-15.11.jpg)
    
    **Figure 15.11:** DNS Lookup Tool
    
4.  **Network mapping and interception tools**: Tools used for mapping, monitoring, and intercepting wired and wireless networks are used for the same purposes in the cloud and IoT environment. Tools like *Nmap*, *Zenmap*, *Netcat*, *Metasploit*, *Nikto,* and *Burp Suite* are widely used on the network level to aid hacking into the cloud and IoT systems.

# [Conclusion](#toc.xhtml#s201a)

Cloud and IoT systems have seen unparalleled growth in recent times and will continue to develop at a very rapid pace. At their core, the computing concepts however remain the same and so do the vulnerabilities. The addition of multiple interaction points increases attack surfaces, and hence it becomes very important to analyze the systems thoroughly prior to release.

One of the streams of study that aids in securing these systems effectively is Cryptography. The process of encryption and hashing goes a long way into securing information not only in the traditional systems but also in the modern cloud and IoT platforms. We will be covering these concepts in detail along with practical implementations of cryptography in the next chapter.

# [Test Your Understanding](#toc.xhtml#s202a)

1.  **A financial conglomerate decided to move to the cloud. The data is highly sensitive and private. Which cloud deployment should they go with if data security is of the utmost concern?** 
    1.  Public
    2.  Private
    3.  Community
    4.  Hybrid
2.  **Which of the following is not a server virtualization product?** 
    1.  VMware ESXi
    2.  Nutanix AOS
    3.  Citrix Hypervisor
    4.  MSI Afterburner
3.  **Which of the following contains a cloud security standard specifically for payment interfaces?** 
    1.  HIPPA
    2.  ISO 27001/ISO 27002
    3.  PIC DSS
    4.  ISO 27017
4.  **What is the open-source IoT middleware for integrating varying sensors using a set of abstractions called?** 
    1.  Open Middleware Standards
    2.  Global Connected Networks
    3.  Open Connected Systems
    4.  Global Sensor Networks
5.  **Which of the following search engines is specifically built for searching IoT devices?** 
    1.  Bing
    2.  Google
    3.  Shodan
    4.  OneSearch

# [Answers](#toc.xhtml#s203a)

1.  **b**
2.  **d**
3.  **c**
4.  **d**
5.  **c**

# [CHAPTER 16](#toc.xhtml#c16)

# [Cryptography](#toc.xhtml#c16)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml#s204a)

The need for secrecy in information traversal from one point to another has been present throughout history. One of the earliest known forms of cryptography in the known history was the Caesar cipher which was used by the Romans to encrypt war-time messages being sent across to the field.

From using simple block and substitution ciphers to much more sophisticated cryptographic algorithms, the field of cryptography has been evolving with the need for privacy and secrecy.

# [Structure](#toc.xhtml#s205a)

We will cover the following topics in this chapter:

*   Basics of cryptography
*   Encryption standards
*   Public-key infrastructure and SSL
*   Digital signatures
*   Hashing
*   Cryptanalysis

# [Objectives](#toc.xhtml#s206a)

This chapter deals with the art and science of hiding data by processing it against procedures to make it ineligible for unwarranted parties during communication and storage. Going through the chapter, the readers will gain a basic understanding of cryptographic practices and their applications. By the end of the chapter, you should be able to identify the need for cryptography and identify and distinguish between different solutions needed for varying use cases in the information systems.

# [Cryptographic primitives](#toc.xhtml#s207a)

Cryptography is the practice of securing data and communication against unsolicited access and modification. The main objective of a cryptographic function is to convert data into a format that cannot be understood by the interceptor but can be easily interpreted by the intended receiver. Such data is referred to as the cipher. The underlying belief being that no matter how secure a channel is, there is bound to be leakage. A cryptographic function thus must necessarily have the following qualities:

*   It must be secure so that it is not easy for the interceptor to make sense of the message.
*   It must be cost-optimal since cryptographic operations cost CPU computation cycles and other resources.
*   It must be time-efficient since data is important when it is needed. Stale data has no value and hence, if a security measure does not allow timely access of data, the security will have no real consequence.

![](images/Figure-16.1.jpg)

**Figure 16.1:** Cryptography trade-offs

Perhaps one of the oldest practical uses of cryptography was implemented by the Romans in the Caesar Cipher. The technique, also known as **shifting cipher**, replaces each character of the language with a character of a fixed length distance from it, the distance being called the shift. For the English language, the shift of 2 would mean the cipher matching to the one in *[figure 16.2](#c16.xhtml#fig16_2)*.

![](images/Figure-16.2.jpg)

**Figure 16.2:** Caesar cipher

The cipher above will hence transform a given sentence as follows:

Plain message: A QUICK BROWN FOX.

Ciphertext: C SWKEM DTQYP HQZ.

Though seemingly easy to crack, Caesar Cipher is still popular for its simplicity and holds academic importance in the world of cryptography. It lays down the foundation of many more complex and shifting transformational techniques. However, it was not until the **development of the public encryption standard** (**DES**) and the invention of public-key cryptography that the field gained importance in the public domain.

Today, cryptography can be largely studied under two sections based on the use of keys to obtain the output from a cryptographic function:

1.  Symmetric key cryptography
2.  Public-key cryptography

Symmetric **k**ey **c**ryptography: The use of a single cryptographic key between the sender and the receiver for encryption and decryption is known as **symmetric** or **private** **key cryptography**. Both sender and receiver have the copy of the same key, which when used in the encryption function, returns a ciphertext, and when utilized in a mirrored decryption function, gives out the original message. Since the keys need to be passed on from the sender to the receiver, the security of the key itself becomes imperative. Thus, transmitting keys along with the message is avoided and generally, a pre-agreed key is used.

![](images/Figure-16.3.jpg)

**Figure 16.3:** Symmetric key cryptography

The symmetric key algorithms, based on their mode of processing the data, are classified as follows:

*   **Stream algorithms**: The encryption takes place on the data instantaneously present in the system, taking in data usually one byte at a time. These are fast and more suitable for hardware implementation. 
    
    ![](images/Figure-16.4.jpg)
    
    **Figure 16.4:** Stream cipher generation
    
*   **Block algorithms**: The encryption takes place on the fixed blocks of data sets. The system hence first breaks the data to be encrypted into fixed block sizes that are then encrypted. These are easier to implement in software but are slower than the stream ciphers. 
    
    ![](images/Figure-16.5.jpg)
    
    **Figure 16.5:** Block Cipher Generation
    

Some common symmetric key algorithms include:

*   **DES**: Data Encryption Standard, Block Cipher
*   **AES**: Advanced Encryption Standard, Block Cipher
*   **Blowfish**: Block Cipher
*   **RC4**: Rivest Cipher 4, Stream Cipher

**Asymmetric key cryptography**: The process of using different and mathematically connected keys for encryption and decryption is known as **asymmetric** or **public-key cryptography**. The key set hence comprises of two types of keys, the public-key which is openly distributed by the receiver to the senders for encryption of the message, and the private key which is only known to the intended decryption owner. Thus, the process eliminates the need to transmit decryption keys since these are no longer used in encryption.

![](images/Figure-16.6.jpg)

**Figure 16.6:** Asymmetric Key Cryptography

Asymmetric key cryptography has two major uses in modern cryptosystems:

*   **Public-key encryption**: Perhaps the most important use case for asymmetric key cryptography lies in public-key encryption. The public-key encryption allows for the receiver to openly distribute and use the public-key for encryption purposes without the fear of the decryption process being compromised. Once the message reaches the intended receiver, it can be decrypted by the private key present only with the intended party. The most common example of this is the SSL encryption being used on the world wide web at large. It is a system that runs with multiple public-key exchanges and authorities operating on the public-key cryptography principles. 
    
    ![](images/Figure-16.7.jpg)
    
    **Figure 16.7:** Public-key Infrastructure
    

**Hashing**: While encryption aims to provide a mechanism for the retrieval of a message on transfer, hashing is a one-way mechanism of processing a message against a mathematical function such that it produces a unique digest of a fixed/variable length against the message which cannot be reverted to the original message. Hashing is hence not a means of encryption but serves the purpose of providing unique identifiers for messages. A few important properties of hash functions are as follows:

*   **Deterministic**: A hash function must always return the same hash value for the same input.
*   **Collision**: A hash function should result in a minimum collision, i.e., the probability of two different inputs resulting in the same hash value must be minimal.
*   **Distribution**: A hash function should distribute the hash values evenly against the hash buckets.

Hashing has found usage in a variety of applications like:

*   **Credential storage**: Passwords are stored in databases after hashing, thereby making it very hard to obtain the original passwords even in case of a data breach.
*   **Data validation**: Hash values can be used to validate the contents of a document. The sender can attach the hash value of the message which can then be matched by the receiver by generating the hash value of the received message and comparing the received hash with the generated hash.
*   **Indexing**: An even distribution hash function can be used to index values by their hash value buckets for faster search and retrieval. There are, in fact, very widely used data structures based on hashing and the resultant indexing.

Hash collision is a case where two or more inputs to a hash function result in the same hash value. A hash function is generally designed to avoid collision as far as possible. This is ensured by choosing a hash function that can generate hash values with approximately equal distribution and of sizes appropriate to the estimated variations. For example, if the expected number of queries to be hashed is 100, the hash function should at least be able to generate 100 values. This tight sizing will however make it difficult to design the hash function to avoid a collision. Hence, the hash values would generally be distributed over a much larger space than the count of the values involved.

*   **Digital signatures**: The digital signature, just like a physical signature, is used to verify the authenticity of a document or message. To sign a document with a digital signature, the sender has to generate a key pair for public and private keys using an appropriate algorithm. The hash of the message or the document is then encrypted via the private key. The encrypted message is sent over to the receiving party that also has the public-key of the sender. Upon receiving a digitally signed message, the receiver processes it with the signature verifier with the public-key, which verifies whether the message is authentic or has undergone any modification. 
    
    ![](images/Figure-16.8.jpg)
    
    **Figure 16.8:** Digital signatures
    

**Steganography**: Steganography is the technique of hiding information within another set of information, usually a document within another document or file. This can digitally be achieved in a wide variety of ways such as:

![](images/Figure-16.9.jpg)

**Figure 16.9:** Steganography

*   Hiding bits of information as a pattern in the byte formation of a lossy image.
*   Concealing image frames within high frame rate videos.
*   Encoding information into sound files.
*   Appending data into the unused sections of a file.
*   Embedding messages in text files in patterns.

The point of consideration in steganography is that the transfer medium should ideally not get altered in a significant and observable manner to entice suspicion.

**Cryptanalysis**: With so many encryption techniques in play, one would think that there are plenty of ways to secure information. Cryptanalysis works to break this notion. It is the technique of analyzing cryptographic functions, ciphertexts, and other data in order to uncover the underlying information. The agenda of the process is to break the cryptographic functions like encryption or hashing without the knowledge of the cryptographic key.

While attempting to break a cryptographic system, one of the following is usually true for an attacker:

*   The attacker knows the encryption algorithm but has no knowledge of the key.
*   The attacker knows the key but is oblivious to the encryption algorithm.
*   The attacker has access to some decrypted pair of information against its encrypted part.
*   The attacker has a considerable size of encrypted data, has a sense of what type of information was encrypted but has no decrypted pair.
*   The attacker has the hash values but has no knowledge of the hash function.
*   The attacker has the hash, knows the hash function but is not aware of the salt component.
*   The attacker has some sense of the processes involved or the execution cycles utilized to perform the cryptographic function.

In any of the cases above, the attacker works to obtain recognizable patterns and brute forces against probable cryptographic functions. For example, in cases of hashed password leaks, the attacker analyzes the hashing patterns against the **known hashing** mechanisms to draw out similarities with the obtained hashes.

Similarly, the attacker can work on mapping the known decrypted information against the corresponding ciphertext as in the **chosen-ciphertext attacks**. It can then be used to analyze and identify similar occurrences to iteratively decrypt larger sets of data.

One-time pads have for long been considered as unbreakable ciphers. What exactly makes them unbreakable is the strict process of encryption followed:

1.  The encryption key is of the length equal to the message itself.
2.  The encryption key generated is truly random.
3.  The sender and the receiver both have copies of the key and it is not transferred with the message.
4.  A key once used is not used again whether wholly or partially.

![](images/Figure-16.10.jpg)

**Figure 16.10:** One-time pad

Suppose Bob wants to send a one-time pad secured message to Adam. The process will be as follows:

1.  Bob generates the message to be sent.
2.  Bob also prepares a truly random collection of keys of the same length as the message and sends a copy securely to Adam.
3.  Now, Bob performs some secure operations like logical XOR operations between the message and the keys, bit by bit.
4.  On successful creation of the cipher, Bob sends the cipher to Adam.
5.  Adam matches the one-time pad with the message and extracts the message by performing reverse operations on the received message as per the one-time pad.

Since the keys are truly random, an attacker who is able to intercept a part of the message will find it impossible to detect the true plain text against a ciphertext. And since the one-time pad is not going to be used again, successfully analyzing one part does not expose the other parts of the message or any subsequent message.

**Meet-in-the-middle attacks** are similar to the brute force attacks, where the attacker tries encryption from the plain text end and decryption from the ciphertext end and tries to advance from both paths to meet in the middle.

**Side-channel attacks** tend to analyze cryptographic system properties and their variations by monitoring the cryptographic function getting executed for varying data sets. The information obtained can be used to map the properties of the underlying function, such as the time complexity of the underlying algorithm. This can help narrow down the possible candidates for trials. A similar attack technique, known as the **timing attack**, utilizes the execution time for operations to analyze the algorithm.

# [Cryptanalysis tools](#toc.xhtml#s208a)

Cryptanalysis is a resource-intensive process that improves with the advancement in hardware configurations. Rainbow tables, for example, contain huge dictionaries of cipher and plain text pairs. Similarly, there are many online tools available to analyze ciphers against messages.

**CRANK**: CRANK stands for "*CRyptANalysis toolKit*", and it aims to provide a flexible and efficient environment for solving traditional (pen-and-paper) ciphers with as much automation as possible. Monoalphabetic substitutions, in which each letter of the alphabet is continuously mapped to another (usually different) letter in the text, are examples of traditional ciphers.

![](images/Figure-16.11.jpg)

**Figure 16.11:** Crank interface

**CrypTool**: CrypTool helps you to learn encryption and decryption algorithms in a pleasant environment and bring your cryptography and cryptanalysis skills into effect.

They reflect both classic and modern cryptosystems according to the algorithms included. The triple DES, Blowfish, and RC4 are examples of modern methods, while the Enigma algorithm, the Caesar cipher, and the double-column transposition are examples of classic methods.

The HMacMD5 algorithm can be used to generate a text authentication code, AES can be used to encrypt and decrypt messages, and SHA1 can be used to generate pseudo-random data.

![](images/Figure-16.12.jpg)

**Figure 16.12:** Cryptool interface

**EverCrack**: EverCrack is a GPL-licensed, open-source cryptanalysis engine capable of performing cryptanalysis on monoalphabetic substitutions and transposition ciphers. It can currently crack up to 4000 words in milliseconds, growing in speed as the size of the ciphertext grows larger, making it an efficient *O(log n)* algorithm.

![](images/Figure-16.13.jpg)

**Figure 16.13:** Evercrack Interface

It can also decrypt monoalphabetic substitution ciphers in English, German, French, Spanish, Italian, Dutch, Swedish, and Portuguese, among other languages.

![](images/Figure-16.14.jpg)

**Figure 16.14:** EverCrack Decryption Interface

# [Conclusion](#toc.xhtml#s209a)

Cryptography, in general, has found its application in all sorts of information exchange and storage processes. It should however be understood that merely encrypting a piece of information will not guarantee security. Each piece of information is unique in terms of what it signifies, its worth, and its properties, and would require a customized cryptographic standard for it. You cannot encrypt a missile launch code with box cipher and hope for the best. Similarly, it would be impractical to encrypt your kitchen door lock key with military-grade encryption. As with all security measures, cryptography too requires a delicate balance between cost, effort, and usability.

With this, we have covered the basics of the hack cycle as well as the methodologies and tools required to get into a system and gain information. In the next chapter, we will get familiarized with the art of evading security measures and concealing an intrusion. It will cover the importance of keeping the hack or breach undetected for as long as possible and of circumventing the security measures rather than trying to disable them.

# [Multiple Choice Questions](#toc.xhtml#s210a)

1.  **Which of the following is not a part of the public-key infrastructure?** 
    1.  Verifier
    2.  Issuing authority
    3.  Certificate authority
    4.  Cracker
2.  **Hash functions should exhibit which of the following properties?** 
    1.  Even distribution of hash values
    2.  Low collision
    3.  Both a and b
    4.  Only b
3.  **Steganography can be performed on audio files.** 
    1.  True
    2.  False
4.  **DES falls under which cryptographic category?** 
    1.  Stream cipher
    2.  Block cipher
    3.  Hashing
    4.  None of the above
5.  **What are rainbow tables used for?** 
    1.  Analysis of public-private key pairs
    2.  Analysis of stream ciphers
    3.  Analysis of hash values and plain texts
    4.  Analysis of block ciphers

# [Answers](#toc.xhtml#s211a)

1.  **d**
2.  **b**
3.  **a**
4.  **a**
5.  **c**

# [CHAPTER 17](#toc.xhtml#c17)

# [Evading Security Measures](#toc.xhtml#c17)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml#s212a)

Throughout the length of this book, we have discussed various techniques and tools used in gathering information about a system and getting through the security measures to plan an effective attack. We will now move on to understanding how the information systems are secured and monitored, and the possible loopholes in these processes that can allow hackers to go in and perform malicious activities undetected.

# [Structure](#toc.xhtml#s213a)

We will cover the following topics in this chapter:

*   Security measures in the digital world
*   Assessing security requirements and security measures of an environment
*   Threat modelling
*   Intrusion detection system
*   Evading security measures

# [Objectives](#toc.xhtml#s214a)

This chapter discusses the preparation required and the techniques used to avoid getting detected while performing breaches. We will be elaborating the levels of security mechanisms put in place by system owners, their boundaries, and common implementations. You will also be familiarized with threat modeling of a system which is often used by system owners to secure their systems, but can also be used by security researchers to analyze a system and identify its weakness.

By the end of the chapter, you will be able to analyze a system based on its security measures, understand the intrusion detection systems, and have an understanding of how these can be defeated to conceal malicious activities on a system.

# [Security of an information system](#toc.xhtml#s215a)

With the evolution of digital systems and the ever-increasing reliance on them in almost all sectors, the need to secure them can no longer be ignored. The security measures can be classified as per the layer to which it is applied:

1.  Application-level security
2.  Server-level security
3.  Network-level security
4.  Hardware asset and infrastructure security
5.  People security

1.  **Application-level security**: This includes the security measures taken while developing an application to ensure no security loopholes and backdoors are present in the code. This should necessarily include thorough analysis and testing of not just its own code but also of all the third-party libraries and components. This can be achieved with the help of:  
    1.  **Static code analyzers**: Tools like Agnitio, Checkmarx, Sonar \[Qube\], FindBugs, etc. form the first line of analysis with these being equipped for flagging known coding style issues, vulnerabilities, and so on. With plugins readily available for each of these tools, they are quite easy to include the component during the application development and deployment lifecycle. 
        
        Most of these static code analyzers also generate detailed reports that can be distributed within teams and be used as an integral part of the code review process.
        
        ![](images/Figure-17.1.jpg)
        
        **Figure 17.1:** Agnitio interface for static code analysis
        
    2.  **Use of secure standards and frameworks**: This refers to the developers using tried and tested frameworks while coding an application. Knowing the underlying implementation of libraries also helps in avoiding unhandled exceptions and errors. It is always a good practice to use certified code for common functions like authentication rather than reinventing the wheel and developing the whole system again which could lead to the omission of checks, incomplete testing, and so on. Of course, there can be scenarios wherein a fresh development or extensions to the existing code base is required. Such scenarios need to be tested thoroughly. 
        
        Most standard frameworks tend to inherently incorporate features to provide protection against vulnerabilities like session fixation, XSS, and so on. It should, however, be analyzed against the level of threat expected on the application as well as any configurations required to activate such features.
        
        There can always be a checklist with a developer against probable attacks on the application and the preventive measures that can be taken while coding to avoid these.
        
    3.  **Network interaction testing**: The application, prior to being deployed in production, should be tested for network interactions. The evaluation of network interactions can flag any unwanted requests being made from the system to other systems. This is especially imperative in case the application has third-party libraries or uses open frameworks. 
        
        ![](images/Figure-17.2.jpg)
        
        **Figure 17.2:** Microsoft message analyzer
        
    4.  **Graceful error handling**: A very important aspect of proofing an application is to handle the errors and exceptions in execution, hiding the underlying system details, and propagating only the required information to the upstream system. A web application, for example, need not return the exception trace to the browser and should simply return an appropriate error message. This is effective in hiding information such as code structure, server make, and the frameworks being used.
    5.  **Monitoring and log management**: Effective monitoring with tools like *Grafana*, *Kibana*, etc. help map application state and interactions. It becomes easy to flag attacks like DOS and brute forcing. Logs can help pinpoint the pathway being exploited or attacked. Further, in the case of breaches, logs help identify the accounts and services affected.
2.  **Server-level security**: Servers form an inseparable part of the application infrastructure as they host and serve the application and its resources. Even in serverless deployments, there is always some underlying piece of server software that performs the server operations. It hence becomes necessary to secure a server as one would secure the actual application. Securing the servers includes:  
    *   Timely patching the server with any security patches available from the vendor.
    *   Planning server upgrades in advance in event of servers getting out of support.
    *   Hardening of servers to block default configurations and profiles.
    *   Restricting the use of untested add-ons.
    *   Monitoring the server activity for malicious footprints.
    *   Restricting access to servers on the basis of:  
        1.  Authentication
        2.  Network restriction
    *   Logging the server activity in the form of access logs and process logs.
3.  **Network-level security**: Securing networks becomes important since it is where all the communications flow. Measures like encryption and SSL secure communication but the first level of defense should always be of blocking out any unwanted or malicious interaction in the first place. Attacks that try to exploit network bandwidth, such as denial attacks, can be handled at the network layer by blocking out malicious traffic. Similar measures can be taken against specific IP ranges or organizations. Network-level monitoring can also flag attempted attacks, load points and unwanted server interactions over a network. 
    
    Another aspect of network monitoring is to identify the resources connected to a network. It should also include the monitoring of the state of the machine and its peripherals.
    
    Some network level security measures include:
    
    1.  **Firewall**: A firewall is a network component, hardware, or software that monitors the traffic flowing in and out of a network. A firewall can be visualized as a sieve between two systems allowing only specific packets to flow through it. The flow of packets in and out of a firewall is based on configured rules. General firewall rules include: 
        
        ![](images/Figure-17.3.jpg)
        
        **Figure 17.3:** Firewall
        
        Incoming and outgoing IP access rules
        
        1.  Port access rules
        2.  Subnet access rules
        3.  Packet configuration rules
        
        ![](images/Figure-17.4.jpg)
        
        **Figure 17.4:** Windows firewall configuration
        
        Additionally, firewalls can contain trusted parameters or resources that can be used to bypass the access rules.
        
        Specialized firewalls can be configured based on the layers that need to be guarded, such as database firewalls, cloud-based firewalls, network segmentation firewalls, and so on.
        
        Other categorization of firewalls divides them on the basis of the OSI model layer they work on, namely **Packet-Filtering Firewalls** that work on the network layer, **Circuit-level Gateway Firewalls** that work on the session layers, and **Application-level Firewalls** that work on the application level.
        
    2.  **Proxy servers**: Proxy servers are servers used as gateways between two networks. Thus, all traffic flowing between the two networks is monitored and controlled by the proxy server. Depending upon the use, a proxy server can provide a wide range of features like traffic monitoring, bypassing content restrictions, domain restrictions, domain redirections, and so on. Proxy servers are classified as: 
        
        ![](images/Figure-17.5.jpg)
        
        **Figure 17.5:** Proxy server
        
        1.  **Transparent proxy** servers inform the interacting layers about their presence and pass on the original requestor details to the other party.
        2.  **Anonymous proxy** servers inform the interacting layers about their presence but do not pass on the requestor details to the other party.
        3.  **Distorting proxy** servers pass on fake IP addresses to the servers, thereby hiding the requestor details.
        4.  **High anonymity proxy** servers are distorting proxy servers that periodically keep on changing the IP addresses exposed to the servers, thereby maintaining a higher level of anonymity.
    3.  **Load balancers**: These are systems used to distribute load among the underlying servers. A load balancer hence typically maintains the state of the underlying servers and traffic and requests redirection rules. Upon receiving a request, the load balancer forwards the request to one of the underlying servers based on the configured rules. Automatic scaling on the load balancer level can ensure that the system is able to handle a sudden increase in loads. Rules for request rejection in case of queue overloads help in the graceful handling of network failures in case of the underlying servers being overwhelmed with requests.
    4.  **API gateways**: API gateways are proxies handling service requests to the underlying application servers. These can be used for a variety of purposes like SSL termination, protocol translation, application-level context path rules for request forwarding, URI level request forwarding, throttling, requestor authentication, etc. Common API gateways include Amazon API gateway, Kong, etc.
4.  **Hardware and infrastructure security**: The physical security of the hardware assets is as important as the network or software components. Unauthorized accesses are generally performed on premise. Securing infrastructure includes measures like: 
    
    *   Network component whitelisting to prevent malicious systems from joining the network.
    *   Physical access control to restrict the access and use of systems by authorized users only.
    *   System audits to assess the status of the system.
    *   Division of hardware and network into access-level zones to apply uniform policies of access for zones.
    
    ![](images/Figure-17.6.jpg)
    
    **Figure 17.6:** DMZ architecture
    
    A common security practice is to divide the network into public and private zones. This comprises of a **Demilitarized Zone** (**DMZ**) that has all the public servers. These are usually web and mail servers. The DMZ is separated from both the private network and the external network via different firewalls.
    
    Behind another firewall lies the production zone containing the application and database servers. Additionally, a demarcated corporate network which would include the development and testing zones resides in parallel to the production zone with network restrictions in between.
    
5.  **People security**: Corporate espionage is one of the major contributors to the security breaches reported each year. Disgruntled or former users who have a fair understanding of the network and access to it are a serious cause of concern in this regard. This necessitates:  
    *   Access policies based on need-basis access and their timely update
    *   Access log management for tracking
    *   Workforce training and compliance structure for corporate security policies

![](images/logo_1.png) **Honeypots** are special systems impersonating an intended target of a cyberattack. These are used to deflect attacks from legitimate systems and keep the attacker busy. Often, honeypots are used to lure attackers into disclosing identifiable information for risk mitigation and legal proceedings.

Directing an attacker to a honeypot can be done by providing breadcrumbs leading to the server or by simply redirecting the identified malicious traffic towards the honeypot.

# [Threat Modelling](#toc.xhtml#s216a)

![](images/Figure-17.7.jpg)

**Figure 17.7:** Microsoft threat modelling tool

Threat modelling refers to specifying the components of an information system and their interactions so as to identify the possible attack surfaces and vectors. In a secure development lifecycle, threat modelling forms an integral part of the system design and can help mitigate majority of the risks at design and development time itself. There are a lot of tools available in the market that help build visual representations of the interactions and attack surfaces. *SecuriCAD* by *Vanguard*, *Microsoft Threat Modelling Tool*, etc. are some of the widely used tools for threat modelling.

![](images/Figure-17.8.jpg)

**Figure 17.8:** SecuriCAD threat modelling system

# [Intrusion Detection System (IDS)](#toc.xhtml#s217a)

Intrusion detection systems are specially-designed software or hardware components that work to identify unauthorized or illegitimate access to the underlying systems. IDS can be incorporated at various levels in the information systems such as the physical access points, administrative portal access, resource usage, file systems, and so on.

There are three varieties of IDS based on the underlying detection techniques:

*   **Rule-based IDS**: These intrusion detection systems work on specified rules fed to them. These include access rules to a resource which limits the resource access to a set or users or specified time. Rule-based IDS can identify and restrict unauthorized access attempts that violate their rules.
*   **Behavior-based IDS**: Behavioral IDSs work by building identifiable patterns for the users accessing a system. These are based on the training data they collect over time, such as user behavior while accessing a system. The behavior can be features like the pattern of feature access by a user upon login or the typing speed.
*   **Hybrid IDS**: These IDSs employ both rule-based configuration and behavior identifications.

Common IDS tools include *Snort*, *IBM Security Network Intrusion Prevention System*, *Silver Sky,* and *Tipping point*.

# [Intrusion Prevention System (IPS)](#toc.xhtml#s218a)

While the IDSs primarily work to detect malicious activities and raise alerts for actions by the system administrators, the actions taken are often not quick enough to prevent losses. With information systems becoming more and more critical, it is often desired that the system has automated mechanisms for prevention as well as recovery.

The IPSs are deployed to perform mitigation actions in the form of standard operating procedures based on pre-defined rules which may be signature, anomaly, or policy based. An IPS can, for example, block an IP or a complete subnet from accessing the system in case of a DOS attack detection. Similarly, these can detect malicious files while being transmitted by comparing them against the baseline traffic. Vendors like *Palo Alto*, *Forcepoint,* and *Checkpoint* provide various solutions ranging from standard threat management solutions to next-generation firewalls.

# [Evading security measures](#toc.xhtml#s219a)

A major part of penetrating an information system is to identify the security measures and bypass them to gain access. Another important aspect is to prevent the target system from identifying the attacker and malicious traffic to ensure anonymity and maintain access. Each security measure might or might not warrant a specialized evasion technique. This will hence involve the identification of the IDS in place. This is usually done via identification techniques that are similar to server identification techniques like banner grabbing, port scanning, firewalking, etc. Some common evasion measures include:

1.  **Malicious packet or traffic crafting**: This includes modifying the content of the malicious requests to avoid detection. A common example of this technique is when an attacker tries to pass in special characters in inputs by entering their encoded values if the original characters are not accepted by the system. 
    
    Another technique involves packet slicing wherein the attacker does not send the complete malicious packet in one go but in sliced packets that are reconstructed at the target end.
    
2.  **Attacking the IDS directly**: This involves targeting the IDS system directly to render it useless. DOS attacks on the IDS server are a common tactic to overwhelm the IDS and then carry on the attack. A craftier technique is to generate false positives in the intrusion detection system by carrying out half-baked attacks that do not perform any actions but trigger alarms on the IDS. These can then be accompanied by the actual attack which will most likely get hidden among the false positives in the report.
3.  **IP address spoofing**: Modifying the header information and source address bits to specific IP addresses is known as **IP address spoofing**. IP spoofing can be used to trick servers into believing the request is originating from a different source, generally from a different geographical location. This helps in scenarios where the server allows requests only from specific IP addresses or where the attacker wants to conceal the true IP address. An advance technique known as **source routing** involves the attacker being able to specify the route the packet travels through in the network.
4.  **Port forwarding**: Port forwarding or tunnelling is the process of encapsulating one protocol within the other. This enables the user to use a protocol that is not supported by the network. Common instances on tunnelling include HTTP tunnelling where the attacker encapsulates restricted protocols by encapsulating them in HTTP packets. SSH tunnelling can be used in a similar manner to forward other protocols.
5.  **Proxy servers**: Proxy servers can be used by the attacker to access systems not allowed on the actual network of the attacker due to network or firewall restrictions. Working in a way similar to IP spoofing, the target server is unaware of the actual source IP which is shadowed by the proxy server IP. Advanced methods include using multiple proxy servers in series, which is also known as proxy chaining.

**Tor** is one of the most widely used, advanced anonymity proxy system. Using the concept of proxy chaining, the tor network routes the packets through a series of available proxy servers worldwide before landing the request to the actual intended server. This ensures anonymity in terms of the IP address of the source system as it gets altered multiple times before reaching the server. It is very tedious to trace back the request to the original source, more so in the case of routing being done through different nationalities.

# [Detection by Association](#toc.xhtml#s220a)

An important methodology of tracing requests back to the actual source especially in the case of spoofing or the use of proxy addresses is by association. How this works is digital systems usually respond with payloads with tracking parameters like sessions and cookies. These can get associated with the user if the same session gets active while the evasion system is not being used. Let us understand this with the help of an example.

Bob sets up a large proxy chain to carry out an attack on *abcxyz.com*. Once the proxy chain is live, Bob visits *abcxyz.com* and proceeds to carry out the attack. *abcxyz.com* in the meantime drops a session object in the browser as part of the general practice for the website to provide a customized user experience.

Post attack execution, Bob closes the browser but fails to realize the session object. Next day, he opens the browser and visits *abcxyz.com* without proxy to check the impact of his attack. *abcxyz.com* realizes that there is already a session object in Bob’s browser that associates his current activity with the same session object. Thus, *abcxyz.com* can now associate the attack with Bob and extract his IP address from the current session.

Similar techniques are widely used in much more sophisticated implementation to catch cyber criminals. Analysis include association by browsing habits, packet analysis, absence of activity over some period, network connections, serial user of the accounts, and so on.

What these warrants is a very careful usage of the evading techniques and to keep the testing assets separate from the personal assets, including mail boxes and social accounts.

# [Conclusion](#toc.xhtml#s221a)

Keeping the intrusion undetected while executing a malicious activity is of paramount importance, especially if one needs to exploit the system over a long period of time. The requirement of anonymity is also a no-brainer unless personalized statements are to be made via the hacks. In cases where huge amount of information is to be extracted, or where there are multiple stages to a hack involving repeated interactions with the target environment, detection can shut down the whole operation.

Alternatively, for a system owner, the knowledge of evasive techniques and processes enable them to be better prepared and help plug the leaks before any impact. Tools like threat modelers and analyzers go a long way into helping architects pre-emptively design security solutions for their systems rather than having to take them up post system deployment.

Having completed the hack cycle and all the theoretical concepts, it is now time for us to get into some practical action. The next chapter will present to you some practical tasks that would put you in real-life penetration testing scenarios.

# [Test Your Understanding](#toc.xhtml#s222a)

1.  **Firewalls can be identified by which of the following methods?** 
    1.  Banner grabbing
    2.  Firewalking
    3.  Port scanning
    4.  All of the above
2.  **Proxy servers are used for which of the following purposes?** 
    1.  IP address hiding
    2.  Traffic monitoring
    3.  Both a and b
    4.  None of the above
3.  **Honeypots are used for?** 
    1.  Gracefully handling server errors for end users
    2.  Securing the network traffic by encapsulation via SSL
    3.  Lure attackers into attacking mimic servers
    4.  Allowing attackers to attack actual production servers to measure impact
4.  **Port forwarding can be used for?** 
    1.  Implementing a protocol not supported by the network for a service
    2.  Blocking ports by the use of a firewall
    3.  Allowing all ports of a server to accept the FTP traffic
    4.  Disallowing all ports of a server from accepting the FTP traffic
5.  **The DMZ infrastructure follows which flow from a public network?** 
    1.  Public Network -> Firewall -> Production -> Firewall ->DMZ
    2.  Public Network -> Firewall -> DMZ -> Corporate Network -> Firewall
    3.  Public Network -> Firewall -> Corporate Network -> Firewall -> DMZ
    4.  Public Network -> Firewall -> DMZ -> Firewall -> Production

# [Answers](#toc.xhtml#s223a)

1.  **d**
2.  **c**
3.  **c**
4.  **a**
5.  **d**

# [CHAPTER 18](#toc.xhtml#c18)

# [Practical Exercises on Penetration Testing and Malware Attacks](#toc.xhtml#c18)

While Certified Ethical Hacker has a separate course, CEH - Practical, dealing with the practical lab work on the various tools and techniques covered throughout the curriculum, we will try to cover some basic ones here to get a decent idea and hold of the tools discussed in this book.

# [Structure](#toc.xhtml#s224a)

We will cover the following in this chapter:

*   Exercises and their solution

# [Objectives](#toc.xhtml#s225a)

The following set of exercises will cover some of the basic penetration testing activities using the standard set of tools and their step-by-step execution. While not a requirement for the current course, the major objective is to make the reader comfortable with some of the tools and penetration testing techniques.

# [Exercise 1](#toc.xhtml#s226a)

**Problem Statement**: Map all the devices connected to your local network using nmap.

# [Solution](#toc.xhtml#s227a)

Nmap is a very commonly used network mapping tool. It provides a variety of querying options to scan the network, like subnet filtering, OS scans, ping scans, and so on. It also has a GUI version known as *Zenmap*.

1.  Determine the IP of your system using `ipconfig/ifconfig`. 
    
    ![](images/Figure-18.1.jpg)
    
    **Figure 18.1:** Determine the system IP
    
2.  Open the nmap in the console to verify the installation. 
    
    **Type:** `nmap -h` to display the nmap menu.
    
    If nmap is missing, download it from: **[https://nmap.org/download.html](https://nmap.org/download.html)**.
    
    ![](images/Figure-18.2.jpg)
    
    **Figure 18.2:** Nmap help
    
3.  Perform the nmap scan with `-sn -sP` parameters on the complete IP range for your network to determine the active hosts and their MAC addresses. 
    
    `nmap -sn -sP 192.168.1.1-255`
    
    ![](images/Figure-18.3.jpg)
    
    **Figure 18.3:** Nmap scan for active hosts
    
4.  Perform an OS scan on the discovered machines using the `-O` parameter on a target machine. 
    
    ![](images/Figure-18.4.jpg)
    
    **Figure 18.4:** Nmap scan for OS information
    

List down:

*   All the active IP addresses found in the scan
*   The operating systems determined by the scan

# [Exercise 2](#toc.xhtml#s228a)

**Problem Statement**: Prepare a phishing attack using the *Ghost Phisher* application.

# [Solution](#toc.xhtml#s229a)

Ghost Phisher is a wireless and ethernet security auditing and attack software program created in Python with the Python Qt GUI framework. It can simulate access points and deploy.

1.  Open Ghost Phisher in your Kali system.
2.  If not already present, follow the installation steps at **[https://github.com/savio-code/ghost-phisher](https://github.com/savio-code/ghost-phisher)**. 
    
    ![](images/Figure-18.5.jpg)
    
    **Figure 18.5:** Ghost Phisher interface
    
3.  Navigate to the Fake HTTP server tab.
4.  Set the HTTP interface to Loopback.
5.  Set the IP address to 127.0.0.1 (localhost).
6.  User webpage settings:  
    *   Select the Clone Website option and put in **[http://www.example.com](http://www.example.com)**.
    *   In “*Real IP Address or Website url*”, put in **[http://www.example.com](http://www.example.com)**.
7.  Click on `Start`. 
    
    ![](images/Figure-18.6.jpg)
    
    **Figure 18.6:** Ghost Phisher in capture mode
    
8.  Open the web browser in your system and navigate to 127.0.0.1. 
    
    ![](images/Figure-18.7.jpg)
    
    **Figure 18.7:** Phished website
    
9.  Navigate to the real **[http://www.example.com](http://www.example.com)** to note the similarities and differences.
10.  Navigate back to the Ghost Phisher application to view any captured activity in the console.

Try this with different websites. Do not host this on an external network without the permission of the actual site owner as this amounts to a possible phishing attack and may invite legal consequences.

# [Exercise 3](#toc.xhtml#s230a)

**Problem Statement**: Use John the Ripper to crack an md5 hash password.

# [Solution](#toc.xhtml#s231a)

John the Ripper is a password security auditing and recovery program that is available for a variety of operating systems.

1.  If your system does not have John pre-installed, install it from **[https://github.com/openwall/john](https://github.com/openwall/john)**.
2.  Open the terminal.
3.  Think of a password and get its md5 hash from the terminal using the following command: 
    
    `echo -n <password> | md5sum`
    
    ![](images/Figure-18.8.jpg)
    
    **Figure 18.8:** Creating md5 hash of a password
    
4.  Create a text file and save the hash in it without the trailing space or the dash symbol.
5.  Run John using the following command: 
    
    `john –format=raw-md5 <filename.txt>`
    
    ![](images/Figure-18.9.jpg)
    
    **Figure 18.9:** Initiating John the Ripper crack
    
6.  Wait for the application to complete the crack. 
    
    ![](images/Figure-18.10.jpg)
    
    **Figure 18.10:** John the Ripper crack is complete
    

Note that while doing hash-based attacks, the application might not always crack the exact password but can also determine another phrase with the same hash (hash collision). In real-world scenarios, this is as good as the exact password.
